🇮🇳 മലയാളം ▼

ഓഡിയോ ആംപ്ലിഫയറിൽ ശബ്ദം വല്ലാതെ കൂട്ടുമ്പോൾ പാട്ടിന്റെ അഗ്രങ്ങൾ പരന്ന് വികൃതമാകുന്ന പ്രതിഭാസമാണിത്!

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: ACS-Q17B

Derive the describing function N(X) for a dead-zone non-linearity. Dead-Zone Non-Linearity-ude Describing Function engane derive cheyyam? ഡെഡ്-സോൺ നോൺ-ലീനിയാരിറ്റിയുടെ ഡിസ്ക്രൈബിംഗ് ഫങ്ഷൻ സമവാക്യം കണ്ടെത്തുക

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$N(X) = k - \frac{2k}{\pi} \left[ \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right) + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{D}{X}\right)^2} \right] \quad (X > D)$$

Dead-zone describing function is complementary to saturation: $N(X)_{\text{deadzone}} = k - N(X)_{\text{saturation}}$.

📌 ⚡ Dead-Zone Describing Function
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$N(X) = k - \frac{2k}{\pi}\left[\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right) + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{D}{X}\right)^2}\right] \quad (X > D)$$

Ormavekkan: Input amplitude $X \le D$ aaneel dead-zone kaaranam output zero aayirikkum ($N(X) = 0$). $X > D$ aavumbol mathrame signal kadannupovoo!

⚡ ⚡ ഡെഡ്-സോൺ സമവാക്യം
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$N(X) = k\left[1 - \frac{2}{\pi}\left(\sin^{-1}\frac{D}{X} + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - \frac{D^2}{X^2}}\right)\right]$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: ഇൻപുട്ട് ഡെഡ്-സോണിനേക്കാൾ ($D$) ചെറുതാണെങ്കിൽ ഔട്ട്പുട്ട് പൂജ്യമായിരിക്കും.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

1. Dead-Zone Characteristic:
A dead-zone non-linearity with threshold $D$ and linear slope $k$ is defined by: $$y(t) = \begin{cases} 0, & -D \le x(t) \le D \\ k(x - D), & x(t) > D \\ k(x + D), & x(t) < -D \end{cases}$$

2. Input Excitation:
Apply sinusoidal input $x(t) = X \sin\theta$, where $\theta = \omega t$.
If $X \le D$, the output is identically zero, so $N(X) = 0$.
If $X > D$, output conducts when input exceeds $D$: $\theta_1 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right)$.

3. Fourier Fundamental Coefficient Derivation:
By symmetry, $A_1 = 0$ (no phase shift). The fundamental sine coefficient is: $$B_1 = \frac{4}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} k (X \sin\theta - D) \sin\theta \, d\theta$$ $$B_1 = \frac{4k X}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin^2\theta \, d\theta - \frac{4k D}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin\theta \, d\theta$$ Using standard integrals: $$\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin^2\theta \, d\theta = \left[ \frac{\theta}{2} - \frac{\sin 2\theta}{4} \right]_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} = \left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) - \left(\frac{\theta_1}{2} - \frac{\sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{2}\right)$$ $$\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin\theta \, d\theta = [-\cos\theta]_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} = \cos\theta_1$$

Substitute $D = X \sin\theta_1$: $$B_1 = \frac{4k X}{\pi} \left[ \frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\theta_1}{2} + \frac{\sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{2} \right] - \frac{4k X \sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{\pi}$$ $$B_1 = k X - \frac{2k X}{\pi} \left[ \theta_1 + \sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1 \right]$$

4. Final Describing Function:
$$N(X) = \frac{B_1}{X} = k \left( 1 - \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right) + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{D}{X}\right)^2} \right] \right) \quad \text{for } X > D$$ $$N(X) = 0 \quad \text{for } X \le D$$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide

1. Angle $\theta_1 = \sin^{-1}(D/X)$. Between $0$ and $\theta_1$, output is $0$.

2. For $\theta_1 \le \theta \le \pi/2$, output is $k(X \sin\theta - D)$.

3. Fundamental integral: $B_1 = \frac{4}{\pi}\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} k(X \sin\theta - D)\sin\theta d\theta$.

4. Result gives $N(X) = \frac{B_1}{X} = k - \frac{2k}{\pi}[\sin^{-1}(D/X) + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - (D/X)^2}]$.

📝 Exact English Text for University Examination:

1. Dead-Zone Characteristic:
A dead-zone non-linearity with threshold $D$ and linear slope $k$ is defined by: $$y(t) = \begin{cases} 0, & -D \le x(t) \le D \\ k(x - D), & x(t) > D \\ k(x + D), & x(t) < -D \end{cases}$$

2. Input Excitation:
Apply sinusoidal input $x(t) = X \sin\theta$, where $\theta = \omega t$.
If $X \le D$, the output is identically zero, so $N(X) = 0$.
If $X > D$, output conducts when input exceeds $D$: $\theta_1 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right)$.

3. Fourier Fundamental Coefficient Derivation:
By symmetry, $A_1 = 0$ (no phase shift). The fundamental sine coefficient is: $$B_1 = \frac{4}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} k (X \sin\theta - D) \sin\theta \, d\theta$$ $$B_1 = \frac{4k X}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin^2\theta \, d\theta - \frac{4k D}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin\theta \, d\theta$$ Using standard integrals: $$\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin^2\theta \, d\theta = \left[ \frac{\theta}{2} - \frac{\sin 2\theta}{4} \right]_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} = \left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) - \left(\frac{\theta_1}{2} - \frac{\sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{2}\right)$$ $$\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin\theta \, d\theta = [-\cos\theta]_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} = \cos\theta_1$$

Substitute $D = X \sin\theta_1$: $$B_1 = \frac{4k X}{\pi} \left[ \frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\theta_1}{2} + \frac{\sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{2} \right] - \frac{4k X \sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{\pi}$$ $$B_1 = k X - \frac{2k X}{\pi} \left[ \theta_1 + \sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1 \right]$$

4. Final Describing Function:
$$N(X) = \frac{B_1}{X} = k \left( 1 - \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right) + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{D}{X}\right)^2} \right] \right) \quad \text{for } X > D$$ $$N(X) = 0 \quad \text{for } X \le D$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പഠന സഹായി

ഡെഡ്-സോൺ കഴിഞ്ഞുള്ള ഭാഗം മാത്രം ഇന്റഗ്രേറ്റ് ചെയ്ത് ഫങ്ഷൻ കണ്ടെത്തുക.

📝 പരീക്ഷയിൽ എഴുതേണ്ട ഇംഗ്ലീഷ് വാചകങ്ങൾ:

1. Dead-Zone Characteristic:
A dead-zone non-linearity with threshold $D$ and linear slope $k$ is defined by: $$y(t) = \begin{cases} 0, & -D \le x(t) \le D \\ k(x - D), & x(t) > D \\ k(x + D), & x(t) < -D \end{cases}$$

2. Input Excitation:
Apply sinusoidal input $x(t) = X \sin\theta$, where $\theta = \omega t$.
If $X \le D$, the output is identically zero, so $N(X) = 0$.
If $X > D$, output conducts when input exceeds $D$: $\theta_1 = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right)$.

3. Fourier Fundamental Coefficient Derivation:
By symmetry, $A_1 = 0$ (no phase shift). The fundamental sine coefficient is: $$B_1 = \frac{4}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} k (X \sin\theta - D) \sin\theta \, d\theta$$ $$B_1 = \frac{4k X}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin^2\theta \, d\theta - \frac{4k D}{\pi} \int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin\theta \, d\theta$$ Using standard integrals: $$\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin^2\theta \, d\theta = \left[ \frac{\theta}{2} - \frac{\sin 2\theta}{4} \right]_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} = \left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) - \left(\frac{\theta_1}{2} - \frac{\sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{2}\right)$$ $$\int_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} \sin\theta \, d\theta = [-\cos\theta]_{\theta_1}^{\pi/2} = \cos\theta_1$$

Substitute $D = X \sin\theta_1$: $$B_1 = \frac{4k X}{\pi} \left[ \frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{\theta_1}{2} + \frac{\sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{2} \right] - \frac{4k X \sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1}{\pi}$$ $$B_1 = k X - \frac{2k X}{\pi} \left[ \theta_1 + \sin\theta_1 \cos\theta_1 \right]$$

4. Final Describing Function:
$$N(X) = \frac{B_1}{X} = k \left( 1 - \frac{2}{\pi} \left[ \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{D}{X}\right) + \frac{D}{X}\sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{D}{X}\right)^2} \right] \right) \quad \text{for } X > D$$ $$N(X) = 0 \quad \text{for } X \le D$$

Figure: Figure 4.2B: Symmetrical Dead-Zone Non-Linearity Characteristic and Output Waveform

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

Think of an old car steering wheel with play in the middle. If you wiggle the wheel slightly within $\pm D$, the wheels don't turn at all ($N(X) = 0$). Only when you turn past the dead-zone do the front tires start responding. As you make larger and larger turns ($X \gg D$), the loose wiggle becomes negligible, and the steering behaves almost like a normal linear wheel ($N(X) \to k$).

💡 Understand It Simply (Manglish) — Local Campus Analogy
🌴 Local Tone ▼

Car-inte steering wheel alpam thirichalum tyre thiriyatha cheriya loose play undallo — athanu Dead-Zone! Cheriya movements full ignore cheyyum. Pakshe steering nannayi thirichaal ($X > D$) matrame car thiriyoo!

🇮🇳 മലയാളം ▼

സ്റ്റിയറിംഗിന്റെ ലൂസ് പ്ലേ പോലെ ചെറിയ അനക്കങ്ങൾ സിസ്റ്റം പരിഗണിക്കാത്ത അവസ്ഥയാണിത്!

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: ACS-Q18

Explain how the stability and frequency of limit cycles in non-linear feedback systems are determined using describing function and Nyquist criterion. Limit Cycle-nte existence-um stability-um Describing Function vechu engane kandupidikkam? ഡിസ്ക്രൈബിംഗ് ഫങ്ഷൻ ഉപയോഗിച്ച് ലിമിറ്റ് സൈക്കിളുകൾ എങ്ങനെ കണ്ടെത്താം?

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G(j\omega) = -\frac{1}{N(X)}$$

Intersection of linear Nyquist plot $G(j\omega)$ and non-linear curve $-1/N(X)$ determines limit cycle amplitude $X$ and frequency $\omega$.

📌 ⚡ Limit Cycle Criterion & Stability
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$G(j\omega) = -\frac{1}{N(X)} \iff 1 + N(X)G(j\omega) = 0$$

Ormavekkan: Nyquist plot of $G(j\omega)$-um $-1/N(X)$ curve-um intersect cheyyunna point-il limit cycle undaakum! Stable limit cycle aavan: Amplitude $X$ koodumbol operating point $-1/N(X)$ curve vazhi stable region-ilekk (outside $G(j\omega)$ encirclement) pokanam.

📌 ⚡ ലിമിറ്റ് സൈക്കിൾ നിബന്ധന
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$G(j\omega) = -\frac{1}{N(X)}$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: $G(j\omega)$ ഗ്രാഫും $-1/N(X)$ ഗ്രാഫും കൂട്ടിമുട്ടുന്ന ബിന്ദുവിൽ ലിമിറ്റ് സൈക്കിൾ രൂപപ്പെടുന്നു.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

1. Limit Cycle Condition:
Consider a closed-loop system with linear plant $G(s)$ in feedback with a non-linear element $N(X)$.
The characteristic equation of the harmonic balance is: $$1 + N(X) G(j\omega) = 0 \implies G(j\omega) = -\frac{1}{N(X)}$$

2. Graphical Determination:

  • Plot the Nyquist polar plot of the linear plant $G(j\omega)$ for $\omega: 0 \to \infty$.
  • Plot the critical locus $-\frac{1}{N(X)}$ on the same complex plane as amplitude $X$ varies from $0 \to \infty$.
  • Limit Cycle Existence: If the two curves intersect at a point $P$, a limit cycle exists!
    • The frequency of the limit cycle $\omega_0$ is read from the $G(j\omega)$ curve at intersection $P$.
    • The amplitude of the limit cycle $X_0$ is read from the $-\frac{1}{N(X)}$ curve at intersection $P$.

3. Stability Criterion for Limit Cycles:

  • According to the extended Nyquist stability criterion, a point is stable if it is not encircled by the $G(j\omega)$ plot.
  • Stable Limit Cycle Criterion: As amplitude $X$ increases along the $-\frac{1}{N(X)}$ curve through intersection point $P$, if the curve moves from the encircled region (unstable zone) to the unencircled region (stable zone), the limit cycle is stable.
    • Physical Explanation: If a disturbance increases $X > X_0$, the system enters the stable region, causing oscillations to decay back to $X_0$. If $X < X_0$, it enters the unstable region, causing oscillations to grow back to $X_0$.
  • Unstable Limit Cycle: If increasing $X$ moves from the unencircled region into the encircled region, any disturbance causes the oscillation either to blow up or collapse to zero.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide

1. Characteristic equation: $1 + N(X)G(j\omega) = 0 \implies G(j\omega) = -\frac{1}{N(X)}$.

2. Polar plot-il $G(j\omega)$ varakkuka. Same graph-il $-\frac{1}{N(X)}$ locus-um varakkuka (with arrow pointing in direction of increasing $X$).

3. Intersection point gives limit cycle frequency $\omega_0$ and amplitude $X_0$.

4. Stability rule: Point $P$-il ninnu $X$ kuranjaal point unstable region-il (inside enclosure) varunnu, so oscillation valuthavunnu. $X$ koodiyaal stable region-il varunnu, so oscillation kurayunnu $\implies$ Stable Limit Cycle!

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

A limit cycle is like your home refrigerator clicking on and off at regular intervals. It settles into a permanent rhythmic heartbeat. Describing function analysis finds the exact meeting point between the cooling coils ($G(j\omega)$) and the thermostat switch ($-1/N(X)$), telling you exactly how loud ($X$) and how fast ($\omega$) the refrigerator will cycle.

Module 5

Phase Plane Analysis & Lyapunov Stability

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: ACS-Q19

Explain phase plane analysis, phase trajectory construction, and classification of singular points in second-order autonomous systems. Phase Plane, Phase Trajectories, and Phase Portrait concepts explain cheyyuka. ഫേസ് പ്ലെയിൻ, ഫേസ് ട്രാജക്ടറി, ഫേസ് പോർട്രെയിറ്റ് എന്നിവ വിശദീകരിക്കുക

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{f_2(x_1, x_2)}{f_1(x_1, x_2)}$$

Singular points occur where slope is $0/0$ ($f_1 = f_2 = 0$). Types: Node, Saddle, Focus, Center.

📌 ⚡ Phase Plane Definitions
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$\text{Slope: } \frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{f_2(x_1, x_2)}{f_1(x_1, x_2)}, \quad x_1 = y, \; x_2 = \dot{y}$$

Ormavekkan: Phase Plane = 2-dimensional state plane ($x_1$ vs $x_2$). Phase Trajectory = Time progress cheyyumbol state point varakkunna curve. Phase Portrait = Ella initial conditions-teyum trajectories koodiya family of curves.

⚡ ⚡ ഫേസ് പ്ലെയിൻ സമവാക്യം
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$\frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{\dot{x}_2}{\dot{x}_1}$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: രണ്ടാമത്തെ ഓർഡർ സിസ്റ്റങ്ങളെ ഗ്രാഫിക്കലായി പഠിക്കാൻ ഉപയോഗിക്കുന്ന 2D പ്ലെയിനാണ് ഫേസ് പ്ലെയിൻ.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

1. Phase Plane & Phase Trajectories:

  • The phase plane is a 2D state space where coordinates are the state variables $x_1$ (displacement) and $x_2 = \dot{x}_1$ (velocity).
  • A curve traced by $(x_1(t), x_2(t))$ from an initial state is called a phase trajectory. A collection of trajectories is a phase portrait.
  • Slope of trajectory at any point: $\frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{\dot{x}_2}{\dot{x}_1} = \frac{f_2(x_1, x_2)}{f_1(x_1, x_2)}$.

2. Singular Points (Equilibrium Points):

  • Points where both state velocities are zero: $f_1(x_1, x_2) = 0$ and $f_2(x_1, x_2) = 0$. The slope $\frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{0}{0}$ is indeterminate.
Singular Point Eigenvalues of Linearized Matrix $A$ Stability Nature
Stable Node Real, unequal, both negative ($\lambda_2 < \lambda_1 < 0$). Asymptotically stable; trajectories enter point without oscillation.
Unstable Node Real, unequal, both positive ($\lambda_1 > \lambda_2 > 0$). Unstable; trajectories diverge outward to infinity.
Saddle Point Real, opposite signs ($\lambda_1 > 0, \lambda_2 < 0$). Always unstable; trajectories approach along one axis and shoot away.
Stable Focus Complex conjugate with negative real part ($\sigma \pm j\omega, \sigma < 0$). Stable; trajectories spiral inward toward the origin.
Center (Vortex) Purely imaginary ($\pm j\omega, \sigma = 0$). Marginally stable; trajectories form closed concentric ellipses (undamped oscillation).

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide
  • Phase Plane: Second-order systems-ine graphically study cheyyan ulla 2D plane ($x_1$ on x-axis, $x_2 = \dot{x}_1$ on y-axis).
  • Phase Trajectory: Oru particular initial condition $(x_1(0), x_2(0))$-il ninnu system move cheyyumbol state point trace cheyyunna path. Slope $S = \frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{\dot{x}_2}{\dot{x}_1}$.
  • Phase Portrait: Pala initial points-il ninnulla ella trajectories-um koodiya complete visual map. System stable aano unstable aano ennu ഒറ്റ glance-il manasilakkam!

1. Phase Plane & Phase Trajectories:

  • The phase plane is a 2D state space where coordinates are the state variables $x_1$ (displacement) and $x_2 = \dot{x}_1$ (velocity).
  • A curve traced by $(x_1(t), x_2(t))$ from an initial state is called a phase trajectory. A collection of trajectories is a phase portrait.

Stable Node Real, unequal, both negative ($\lambda_2 < \lambda_1 < 0$). Asymptotically stable; trajectories enter point without oscillation.
Unstable Node Real, unequal, both positive ($\lambda_1 > \lambda_2 > 0$). Unstable; trajectories diverge outward to infinity.

മൂന്ന് നിർവ്വചനങ്ങളും അവയുടെ ഗ്രാഫിക്കൽ പ്രാധാന്യവും വ്യക്തമാക്കുക.

📝 പരീക്ഷയിൽ എഴുതേണ്ട ഇംഗ്ലീഷ് വാചകങ്ങൾ:

Stable Node Real, unequal, both negative ($\lambda_2 < \lambda_1 < 0$). Asymptotically stable; trajectories enter point without oscillation.
Unstable Node Real, unequal, both positive ($\lambda_1 > \lambda_2 > 0$). Unstable; trajectories diverge outward to infinity.
Saddle Point Real, opposite signs ($\lambda_1 > 0, \lambda_2 < 0$). Always unstable; trajectories approach along one axis and shoot away.
Stable Focus Complex conjugate with negative real part ($\sigma \pm j\omega, \sigma < 0$). Stable; trajectories spiral inward toward the origin.
Center (Vortex) Purely imaginary ($\pm j\omega, \sigma = 0$). Marginally stable; trajectories form closed concentric ellipses (undamped oscillation).

Figure: Figure 5.1: Classification of Singular Points in Phase Plane (Nodes, Foci, Centers, Saddle Points)

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

Imagine dropping a marble on different terrains: on a bowl, it rolls to the bottom and stops (Stable Node). In a swirling drain, it spirals inward to the center (Stable Focus). On a mountain peak, it rolls off and accelerates away (Unstable Node). On a horse saddle, it rolls towards the middle from the sides, but immediately slips off the front or back (Saddle Point)!

💡 Understand It Simply (Manglish) — Local Campus Analogy
🌴 Local Tone ▼

Oru roller-coaster-te map nokkunnathu pole aanu Phase Portrait: X-axis-il position-um Y-axis-il speed-um varakkunnu. Cart engottu pokum, evide stop aavum ennu aa graph-ile curves nokkiyaal direct aayi parayan pattum!

🇮🇳 മലയാളം ▼

റോലർ കോസ്റ്ററിന്റെ വേഗതയും സ്ഥാനവും അടയാളപ്പെടുത്തിയ ട്രാഫിക് മാപ്പ് പോലെയാണിത്!

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: ACS-Q20

State Sylvester's criterion for testing the positive definiteness of quadratic scalar functions V(x) = x^T P x. Scalar Function-te Sign Definiteness-um Sylvester's Criterion-um explain cheyyuka. സൈൻ ഡെഫിനിറ്റ്നസും സിൽവെസ്റ്റേഴ്സ് മാനദണ്ഡവും (Sylvester's Criterion) വിശദീകരിക്കുക

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x} > 0 \iff \Delta_1 > 0, \Delta_2 > 0, \dots, \Delta_n > 0$$

Sylvester's test: Matrix $P$ is positive definite if all leading principal minors are strictly positive.

📌 ⚡ Sylvester's Criterion
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x} > 0 \iff \Delta_1 > 0, \;\; \Delta_2 > 0, \;\; \dots, \;\; \Delta_n > 0$$

Ormavekkan: Positive Definite: $V(\mathbf{0}) = 0$ and $V(\mathbf{x}) > 0 \;\; \forall \mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$. Sylvester test: Matrix $P$-yude all principal leading minors strictly positive aayirikkannam!

📌 ⚡ സിൽവെസ്റ്റേഴ്സ് ടെസ്റ്റ്
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$\Delta_1 > 0, \quad \Delta_2 > 0, \quad \dots, \quad \Delta_n > 0 \implies \text{Positive Definite}$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: മാട്രിക്സ് $P$-യുടെ എല്ലാ ലീഡിംഗ് പ്രിൻസിപ്പൽ മൈനറുകളും പോസിറ്റീവ് ആണെങ്കിൽ ഫങ്ഷൻ പോസിറ്റീവ് ഡെഫിനിറ്റ് ആണ്.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

1. Sign Definiteness of Scalar Function $V(\mathbf{x})$:

  • Positive Definite: $V(\mathbf{0}) = 0$, and $V(\mathbf{x}) > 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$. (Represents stored energy).
  • Positive Semi-Definite: $V(\mathbf{0}) = 0$, and $V(\mathbf{x}) \ge 0$ for all $\mathbf{x}$. It can equal zero at points other than the origin.
  • Negative Definite: $-V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite ($V(\mathbf{x}) < 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$).
  • Indefinite: $V(\mathbf{x})$ takes both positive and negative values depending on direction of $\mathbf{x}$.

2. Sylvester's Criterion for Quadratic Forms:
Let $V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}$, where $P$ is a real symmetric $n \times n$ matrix: $$P = \begin{bmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} & \dots & p_{1n} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} & \dots & p_{2n} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ p_{n1} & p_{n2} & \dots & p_{nn} \end{bmatrix}$$ $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite if and only if all $n$ leading principal minors $\Delta_k$ of $P$ are strictly positive: $$\Delta_1 = p_{11} > 0$$ $$\Delta_2 = \begin{vmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} \end{vmatrix} > 0$$ $$\Delta_3 = \begin{vmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} & p_{13} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} & p_{23} \\ p_{31} & p_{32} & p_{33} \end{vmatrix} > 0, \quad \dots \quad \Delta_n = \det(P) > 0$$ Negative Definite Condition: Minors alternate in sign: $\Delta_1 < 0, \Delta_2 > 0, \Delta_3 < 0, \dots$

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide

1. Sign Classifications:

  • Positive Definite: $V(\mathbf{x}) > 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$, and $V(\mathbf{0}) = 0$. (Bowl shape).
  • Positive Semi-Definite: $V(\mathbf{x}) \ge 0$. (Can be zero even when $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$).
  • Negative Definite: $V(\mathbf{x}) < 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$.
  • Indefinite: Takes both positive and negative values.

2. Sylvester's Criterion: For $P = \begin{bmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} \end{bmatrix}$, check $\Delta_1 = p_{11} > 0$ and $\Delta_2 = \det(P) = p_{11}p_{22} - p_{12}^2 > 0$.

1. Sign Definiteness of Scalar Function $V(\mathbf{x})$:

  • Positive Definite: $V(\mathbf{0}) = 0$, and $V(\mathbf{x}) > 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$. (Represents stored energy).
  • Positive Semi-Definite: $V(\mathbf{0}) = 0$, and $V(\mathbf{x}) \ge 0$ for all $\mathbf{x}$. It can equal zero at points other than the origin.
  • Negative Definite: $-V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite ($V(\mathbf{x}) < 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$).
  • Indefinite: $V(\mathbf{x})$ takes both positive and negative values depending on direction of $\mathbf{x}$.

2. Sylvester's Criterion for Quadratic Forms:
Let $V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}$, where $P$ is a real symmetric $n \times n$ matrix: $$P = \begin{bmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} & \dots & p_{1n} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} & \dots & p_{2n} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ p_{n1} & p_{n2} & \dots & p_{nn} \end{bmatrix}$$ $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite if and only if all $n$ leading principal minors $\Delta_k$ of $P$ are strictly positive: $$\Delta_1 = p_{11} > 0$$ $$\Delta_2 = \begin{vmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} \end{vmatrix} > 0$$ $$\Delta_3 = \begin{vmatrix} p_{11} & p_{12} & p_{13} \\ p_{21} & p_{22} & p_{23} \\ p_{31} & p_{32} & p_{33} \end{vmatrix} > 0, \quad \dots \quad \Delta_n = \det(P) > 0$$ Negative Definite Condition: Minors alternate in sign: $\Delta_1 < 0, \Delta_2 > 0, \Delta_3 < 0, \dots$

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

Think of $V(\mathbf{x})$ as a physical bowl. If it is positive definite, the bowl curves upward in every direction—the only lowest point is the exact bottom at the origin ($0, 0$). If you drop a ball anywhere inside, it has positive potential energy and will slide toward the center.

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: ACS-Q20B

Explain Krasovskii's method and variable gradient method for the construction of Lyapunov functions. Lyapunov function generate cheyyunna methods (Krasovskii's & Variable Gradient) explain cheyyuka. ലയപുനോവ് ഫങ്ഷനുകൾ നിർമ്മിക്കുന്ന രീതികൾ (Krasovskii's & Variable Gradient) വിശദീകരിക്കുക

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x}) \quad \text{[Krasovskii]}, \quad \nabla V = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}) \implies \frac{\partial g_i}{\partial x_j} = \frac{\partial g_j}{\partial x_i} \quad \text{[Var. Gradient]}$$

Systematic methods to construct energy-like scalar functions $V(\mathbf{x})$ for non-linear stability proofs.

📌 ⚡ Krasovskii's Theorem Formula
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}^T(\mathbf{x}) P \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x}), \quad J(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\partial \mathbf{f}}{\partial \mathbf{x}}, \quad \hat{F} = J^T P + P J < 0$$

Ormavekkan: Non-linear systems-inu Lyapunov function kandupidikkan trial-and-error venda: Krasovskii method Jacobian matrix $J(\mathbf{x})$ use cheyyunnu; Variable gradient method $\nabla V$ assume cheythu line integral edukkunnu.

⚡ ⚡ ക്രസോവ്സ്കി സമവാക്യം
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$\hat{F} = J + J^T < 0 \implies \text{Asymptotically Stable}$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: ജക്കോബിയൻ മാട്രിക്സ് ഉപയോഗിച്ച് നോൺ-ലീനിയർ സിസ്റ്റങ്ങൾക്ക് കൃത്യമായ എനർജി ഫങ്ഷൻ കണ്ടെത്താം.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Constructing a valid Lyapunov function $V(\mathbf{x})$ for non-linear systems $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$ is challenging. Two systematic methods exist:

1. Krasovskii's Method:

  • Proposes a candidate Lyapunov function based on the system vector field $\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$: $$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$$ where $P$ is a symmetric positive definite matrix (often chosen as $P = I$).
  • Define the Jacobian matrix $J(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\partial \mathbf{f}}{\partial \mathbf{x}}$. The derivative is: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}^T \left( J^T P + P J \right) \mathbf{f} = \mathbf{f}^T F(\mathbf{x}) \mathbf{f}$$
  • Theorem: If matrix $F(\mathbf{x}) = J^T P + P J$ is negative definite in a neighborhood around origin, the equilibrium point is asymptotically stable.

2. Variable Gradient Method (Schultz-Gibson Method):

  • Assumes the gradient vector $\nabla V = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}) = \begin{bmatrix} g_1 & g_2 & \dots & g_n \end{bmatrix}^T$.
  • Enforces curl-free integrability conditions: $$\frac{\partial g_i}{\partial x_j} = \frac{\partial g_j}{\partial x_i} \quad \forall i, j$$
  • Computes $\dot{V} = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})^T \dot{\mathbf{x}} = \sum g_i f_i$ and selects coefficients to make $\dot{V}$ negative semi-definite or negative definite.
  • Integrates $\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})$ along a spatial path to reconstruct the scalar Lyapunov function $V(\mathbf{x}) = \int_0^{\mathbf{x}} \mathbf{g}^T d\mathbf{x}$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide

1. Krasovskii's Method: For $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$, form Jacobian $J = \frac{\partial f_i}{\partial x_j}$. Define matrix $\hat{F} = J + J^T$. If $\hat{F}$ is negative definite, then $V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}^T \mathbf{f}$ is a valid Lyapunov function and system is asymptotically stable!

2. Variable Gradient Method: Assume gradient vector $\nabla V = [g_1(\mathbf{x}), g_2(\mathbf{x})]^T$. Apply curl condition $\frac{\partial g_1}{\partial x_2} = \frac{\partial g_2}{\partial x_1}$. Compute line integral $V(\mathbf{x}) = \int_0^{\mathbf{x}} \nabla V \cdot d\mathbf{x}$. Ensure $\dot{V} = \nabla V^T \mathbf{f} \le 0$.

📝 Exact English Text for University Examination:

Constructing a valid Lyapunov function $V(\mathbf{x})$ for non-linear systems $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$ is challenging. Two systematic methods exist:

1. Krasovskii's Method:

  • Proposes a candidate Lyapunov function based on the system vector field $\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$: $$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$$ where $P$ is a symmetric positive definite matrix (often chosen as $P = I$).
  • Define the Jacobian matrix $J(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\partial \mathbf{f}}{\partial \mathbf{x}}$. The derivative is: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}^T \left( J^T P + P J \right) \mathbf{f} = \mathbf{f}^T F(\mathbf{x}) \mathbf{f}$$
  • Theorem: If matrix $F(\mathbf{x}) = J^T P + P J$ is negative definite in a neighborhood around origin, the equilibrium point is asymptotically stable.

2. Variable Gradient Method (Schultz-Gibson Method):

  • Assumes the gradient vector $\nabla V = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}) = \begin{bmatrix} g_1 & g_2 & \dots & g_n \end{bmatrix}^T$.
  • Enforces curl-free integrability conditions: $$\frac{\partial g_i}{\partial x_j} = \frac{\partial g_j}{\partial x_i} \quad \forall i, j$$
  • Computes $\dot{V} = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})^T \dot{\mathbf{x}} = \sum g_i f_i$ and selects coefficients to make $\dot{V}$ negative semi-definite or negative definite.
  • Integrates $\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})$ along a spatial path to reconstruct the scalar Lyapunov function $V(\mathbf{x}) = \int_0^{\mathbf{x}} \mathbf{g}^T d\mathbf{x}$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പഠന സഹായി

ജക്കോബിയൻ മാട്രിക്സും വേരിയബിൾ ഗ്രേഡിയന്റ് ലൈൻ ഇന്റഗ്രലും വിശദീകരിക്കുക.

📝 പരീക്ഷയിൽ എഴുതേണ്ട ഇംഗ്ലീഷ് വാചകങ്ങൾ:

Constructing a valid Lyapunov function $V(\mathbf{x})$ for non-linear systems $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$ is challenging. Two systematic methods exist:

1. Krasovskii's Method:

  • Proposes a candidate Lyapunov function based on the system vector field $\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$: $$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$$ where $P$ is a symmetric positive definite matrix (often chosen as $P = I$).
  • Define the Jacobian matrix $J(\mathbf{x}) = \frac{\partial \mathbf{f}}{\partial \mathbf{x}}$. The derivative is: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{f}^T \left( J^T P + P J \right) \mathbf{f} = \mathbf{f}^T F(\mathbf{x}) \mathbf{f}$$
  • Theorem: If matrix $F(\mathbf{x}) = J^T P + P J$ is negative definite in a neighborhood around origin, the equilibrium point is asymptotically stable.

2. Variable Gradient Method (Schultz-Gibson Method):

  • Assumes the gradient vector $\nabla V = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x}) = \begin{bmatrix} g_1 & g_2 & \dots & g_n \end{bmatrix}^T$.
  • Enforces curl-free integrability conditions: $$\frac{\partial g_i}{\partial x_j} = \frac{\partial g_j}{\partial x_i} \quad \forall i, j$$
  • Computes $\dot{V} = \mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})^T \dot{\mathbf{x}} = \sum g_i f_i$ and selects coefficients to make $\dot{V}$ negative semi-definite or negative definite.
  • Integrates $\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{x})$ along a spatial path to reconstruct the scalar Lyapunov function $V(\mathbf{x}) = \int_0^{\mathbf{x}} \mathbf{g}^T d\mathbf{x}$.

Figure: Figure 5.2B: Gradient Vector Field Integration to Synthesize Lyapunov Function V(x)

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

Finding a Lyapunov function by guessing is like looking for a lost key in the dark. Krasovskii's method builds a key automatically from the system's own equations. The Variable Gradient method works backwards: it draws the slope (gradient) of the energy bowl first to ensure the ball always rolls downward, then reconstructs the bowl by integration.

💡 Understand It Simply (Manglish) — Local Campus Analogy
🌴 Local Tone ▼

Kunnin mukalil ninnu thazhottu vellam ozhukunna path kandupidikkunnathu pole aanu ithu: Krasovskii method slope (Jacobian) check cheythu thazhottanu pokunnath ennu urappakkunnu. Variable gradient method oru compass vechu step-by-step aayi route integrate cheyyunnu!

🇮🇳 മലയാളം ▼

കുന്നിൻ മുകളിൽ നിന്നും താഴേക്ക് പോകുന്ന ചെരിവ് കണക്കുകൂട്ടി സിസ്റ്റം സ്റ്റേബിളാണോ എന്ന് ഉറപ്പുവരുത്തുന്ന വിദ്യ!

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: ACS-Q21

Construct the phase portrait and determine the singular points of a non-linear second-order system using the method of isoclines. Singular Points-nte Classification explain cheyyuka (Node, Saddle, Focus, Center). സിംഗുലർ പോയിന്റുകളുടെ വർഗ്ഗീകരണം വിശദീകരിക്കുക (Node, Saddle, Focus, Center)

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$J = \begin{bmatrix} \partial f_1/\partial x_1 & \partial f_1/\partial x_2 \\ \partial f_2/\partial x_1 & \partial f_2/\partial x_2 \end{bmatrix}_{\mathbf{x}_0}$$

Linearize non-linear system at each equilibrium point $\mathbf{x}_0$ using Jacobian matrix $J$; classify singular points from eigenvalues of $J$.

📌 ⚡ Singular Points by Eigenvalues
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$\text{Node: Real, same sign} \quad \text{Saddle: Real, opposite signs} \quad \text{Focus: Complex conjugates} \quad \text{Center: Purely imaginary}$$

Ormavekkan: $\dot{x}_1 = 0$ and $\dot{x}_2 = 0$ aavunna equilibrium points aanu singular points. Eigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$-nte nature nokki nature direct aayi theerumanikkam!

📌 ⚡ പോയിന്റ് വർഗ്ഗീകരണം
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$\text{Node (Real)}, \quad \text{Focus (Complex)}, \quad \text{Center (Imaginary)}, \quad \text{Saddle (Opposite)}$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: ഐഗൻവാല്യൂകളുടെ സ്വഭാവം നോക്കി സിംഗുലർ പോയിന്റുകളുടെ ടൈപ്പും സ്റ്റെബിലിറ്റിയും കണ്ടെത്തുന്നു.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

1. Non-Linear Autonomous System:
Consider a second-order non-linear system: $$\dot{x}_1 = f_1(x_1, x_2), \quad \dot{x}_2 = f_2(x_1, x_2)$$

2. Step-by-Step Construction Procedure:

  1. Step 1: Determine Equilibrium (Singular) Points:
    Set $\dot{x}_1 = 0$ and $\dot{x}_2 = 0$: $$f_1(x_1, x_2) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad f_2(x_1, x_2) = 0$$ Solve these simultaneous algebraic equations to find singular points $(x_{1e}, x_{2e})$.
  2. Step 2: Linearize around each Singular Point:
    Evaluate the Jacobian matrix at each singular point: $$J = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial f_1}{\partial x_1} & \frac{\partial f_1}{\partial x_2} \\ \frac{\partial f_2}{\partial x_1} & \frac{\partial f_2}{\partial x_2} \end{bmatrix}_{(x_{1e}, x_{2e})}$$ The linearized local dynamics are: $\dot{\mathbf{\Delta x}} = J \mathbf{\Delta x}$.
  3. Step 3: Calculate Eigenvalues & Classify Singular Points:
    Solve characteristic equation $\det(\lambda I - J) = 0 \implies \lambda^2 - \operatorname{Tr}(J)\lambda + \det(J) = 0$:
    • $\det(J) < 0 \implies$ Saddle Point (always unstable).
    • $\det(J) > 0, \operatorname{Tr}(J) < 0, (\operatorname{Tr})^2 - 4\det \ge 0 \implies$ Stable Node.
    • $\det(J) > 0, \operatorname{Tr}(J) > 0, (\operatorname{Tr})^2 - 4\det \ge 0 \implies$ Unstable Node.
    • $\det(J) > 0, \operatorname{Tr}(J) < 0, (\operatorname{Tr})^2 - 4\det < 0 \implies$ Stable Focus (inward spiral).
    • $\det(J) > 0, \operatorname{Tr}(J) = 0 \implies$ Center / Vortex (closed periodic orbit).
  4. Step 4: Sketch Trajectories (Isocline Method):
    Find curves of constant slope $m = \frac{dx_2}{dx_1} = \frac{f_2(x_1, x_2)}{f_1(x_1, x_2)}$. Draw slope direction markers along each isocline.
  5. Step 5: Connect Trajectories:
    Connect directional markers smoothly, respecting arrow directions corresponding to time progression ($\dot{x}_1 > 0 \implies$ move right; $\dot{x}_1 < 0 \implies$ move left).

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide
Singular PointEigenvalues $\lambda_1, \lambda_2$Stability
Stable NodeReal, distinct, both negative ($\lambda_2 < \lambda_1 < 0$)Asymptotically Stable
Unstable NodeReal, distinct, both positive ($\lambda_2 > \lambda_1 > 0$)Unstable
Saddle PointReal, opposite signs ($\lambda_1 < 0 < \lambda_2$)Always Unstable
Stable FocusComplex conjugates with negative real part ($\sigma \pm j\omega, \sigma < 0$)Stable (spirals inward)
Unstable FocusComplex conjugates with positive real part ($\sigma \pm j\omega, \sigma > 0$)Unstable (spirals outward)
Center (Vortex)Purely imaginary ($\pm j\omega, \sigma = 0$)Marginally Stable (concentric circles)

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

Mapping a non-linear phase portrait is like drawing a weather wind map over Kerala. First, you locate the calm eye of the storms (singular points where wind speed is zero). Next, you check if wind spirals into the center (cyclone/focus) or blows away. Then you draw the wind arrows across the map to see the overall flow of weather!

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: ACS-Q22

State and prove Lyapunov's direct method stability theorems for continuous-time autonomous LTI systems using the matrix equation A^T P + P A = -Q. Lyapunov Direct Method-um Matrix Equation $A^T P + P A = -Q$ derivation-um explain cheyyuka. ലയപുനോവിന്റെ ഡയറക്ട് രീതിയും $A^T P + P A = -Q$ സമവാക്യവും വിശദീകരിക്കുക

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$A^T P + P A = -Q, \quad V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x} > 0 \implies \dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = -\mathbf{x}^T Q \mathbf{x} < 0$$

For linear systems $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$: Asymptotically stable if unique positive definite $P$ exists for any positive definite $Q$ (e.g. $Q = I$).

⚡ ⚡ Lyapunov Matrix Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🌴 Manglish Tone
$$A^T P + P A = -Q, \quad V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}, \quad \dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = -\mathbf{x}^T Q \mathbf{x}$$

Ormavekkan: Oru linear system $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$ asymptotically stable aakanamenkil: Any arbitrary positive definite matrix $Q$ (eg: $Q = I$) eduthaal, $A^T P + PA = -Q$ solve cheyyumbol kittunna matrix $P$-yum strictly positive definite aayirikkannam!

⚡ ⚡ ലയപുനോവ് മാട്രിക്സ് സമവാക്യം
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time 🇮🇳 മലയാളം
$$A^T P + P A = -Q$$

ഓർത്തു വെക്കാൻ: പോസിറ്റീവ് ഡെഫിനിറ്റ് ആയ $Q$-ന് ലഭിക്കുന്ന $P$ പോസിറ്റീവ് ഡെഫിനിറ്റ് ആണെങ്കിൽ സിസ്റ്റം അസിംപ്റ്റോട്ടിക്കലി സ്റ്റേബിൾ ആണ്.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

1. Lyapunov's Direct (Second) Method Theorems:
Consider an autonomous system $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$ with equilibrium at the origin $\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{0}) = \mathbf{0}$. Let $V(\mathbf{x})$ be a continuously differentiable scalar function:

  • Theorem 1 (Stability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite ($V(\mathbf{0})=0, V(\mathbf{x})>0$ for $\mathbf{x}\ne\mathbf{0}$) and its time derivative is negative semi-definite ($\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) \le 0$), the origin is stable in the sense of Lyapunov.
  • Theorem 2 (Asymptotic Stability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite and its time derivative is strictly negative definite ($\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) < 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$), the origin is asymptotically stable.
  • Theorem 3 (Instability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite and $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x})$ is strictly positive definite, the origin is unstable.

2. Application to Linear Systems ($\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$):

Choose a quadratic candidate Lyapunov function: $$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}$$ where $P$ is an $n \times n$ real symmetric positive definite matrix ($P = P^T, P > 0$).

3. Derivation of the Lyapunov Matrix Equation:
Take the time derivative of $V(\mathbf{x})$ along system trajectories: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \dot{\mathbf{x}}^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P \dot{\mathbf{x}}$$ Substitute $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$ and $\dot{\mathbf{x}}^T = \mathbf{x}^T A^T$: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = (A\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P (A\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T A^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P A \mathbf{x}$$ $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T \left( A^T P + P A \right) \mathbf{x}$$ For asymptotic stability, we require $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x})$ to be negative definite: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = -\mathbf{x}^T Q \mathbf{x} < 0$$ where $Q$ is any chosen positive definite symmetric matrix (commonly $Q = I$).

Equating terms yields the famous Lyapunov Matrix Equation: $$A^T P + P A = -Q$$

4. Verification Procedure:

  1. Choose an arbitrary positive definite matrix $Q$ (usually the identity matrix $Q = I$).
  2. Solve the linear matrix equation $A^T P + P A = -Q$ for symmetric matrix $P$.
  3. Test matrix $P$ using Sylvester's criterion: if all leading principal minors of $P$ are strictly positive ($P > 0$), the linear system is asymptotically stable.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

🌴 Manglish Study Guide

Step-by-step Derivation:

  1. Choose quadratic candidate energy: $V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}$, with $P = P^T > 0$.
  2. Time derivative: $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \dot{\mathbf{x}}^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P \dot{\mathbf{x}}$.
  3. Substitute $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$: $\dot{V} = (A\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P (A\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T A^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P A \mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x}^T (A^T P + P A) \mathbf{x}$.
  4. For asymptotic stability, we require $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = -\mathbf{x}^T Q \mathbf{x} < 0$, where $Q > 0$.
  5. Equating both terms gives the celebrated Lyapunov equation: $A^T P + P A = -Q$.
📝 Exact English Text for University Examination:

1. Lyapunov's Direct (Second) Method Theorems:
Consider an autonomous system $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = \mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})$ with equilibrium at the origin $\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{0}) = \mathbf{0}$. Let $V(\mathbf{x})$ be a continuously differentiable scalar function:

  • Theorem 1 (Stability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite ($V(\mathbf{0})=0, V(\mathbf{x})>0$ for $\mathbf{x}\ne\mathbf{0}$) and its time derivative is negative semi-definite ($\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) \le 0$), the origin is stable in the sense of Lyapunov.
  • Theorem 2 (Asymptotic Stability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite and its time derivative is strictly negative definite ($\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) < 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$), the origin is asymptotically stable.
  • Theorem 3 (Instability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite and $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x})$ is strictly positive definite, the origin is unstable.

2. Application to Linear Systems ($\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$):

Choose a quadratic candidate Lyapunov function: $$V(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}$$ where $P$ is an $n \times n$ real symmetric positive definite matrix ($P = P^T, P > 0$).

3. Derivation of the Lyapunov Matrix Equation:
Take the time derivative of $V(\mathbf{x})$ along system trajectories: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \dot{\mathbf{x}}^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P \dot{\mathbf{x}}$$ Substitute $\dot{\mathbf{x}} = A\mathbf{x}$ and $\dot{\mathbf{x}}^T = \mathbf{x}^T A^T$: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = (A\mathbf{x})^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P (A\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T A^T P \mathbf{x} + \mathbf{x}^T P A \mathbf{x}$$ $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{x}^T \left( A^T P + P A \right) \mathbf{x}$$ For asymptotic stability, we require $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x})$ to be negative definite: $$\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) = -\mathbf{x}^T Q \mathbf{x} < 0$$ where $Q$ is any chosen positive definite symmetric matrix (commonly $Q = I$).

Equating terms yields the famous Lyapunov Matrix Equation: $$A^T P + P A = -Q$$

4. Verification Procedure:

  1. Choose an arbitrary positive definite matrix $Q$ (usually the identity matrix $Q = I$).
  2. Solve the linear matrix equation $A^T P + P A = -Q$ for symmetric matrix $P$.
  3. Test matrix $P$ using Sylvester's criterion: if all leading principal minors of $P$ are strictly positive ($P > 0$), the linear system is asymptotically stable.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പഠന സഹായി

എനർജി ഫങ്ഷൻ $V = \mathbf{x}^T P \mathbf{x}$-ന്റെ ഡെറിവേറ്റീവ് എടുത്ത് $A^T P + P A = -Q$ തെളിയിക്കുക.

📝 പരീക്ഷയിൽ എഴുതേണ്ട ഇംഗ്ലീഷ് വാചകങ്ങൾ:

1. Lyapunov's Direct (Second) Method Theorems:

  • Theorem 2 (Asymptotic Stability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite and its time derivative is strictly negative definite ($\dot{V}(\mathbf{x}) < 0$ for all $\mathbf{x} \ne \mathbf{0}$), the origin is asymptotically stable.
  • Theorem 3 (Instability): If $V(\mathbf{x})$ is positive definite and $\dot{V}(\mathbf{x})$ is strictly positive definite, the origin is unstable.

Equating terms yields the famous Lyapunov Matrix Equation: $$A^T P + P A = -Q$$

4. Verification Procedure:

  1. Choose an arbitrary positive definite matrix $Q$ (usually the identity matrix $Q = I$).
  2. Solve the linear matrix equation $A^T P + P A = -Q$ for symmetric matrix $P$.
  3. Test matrix $P$ using Sylvester's criterion: if all leading principal minors of $P$ are strictly positive ($P > 0$), the linear system is asymptotically stable.

Figure: Figure 5.4: State Trajectory Penetrating Inward across Lyapunov Contours V(x) = C

💡 Understand It Simply — Intuitive Explanation & Analogy
Learning Aid ▼

Lyapunov's direct method is the universal "energy test" for stability. If a mechanical system has positive stored energy ($V > 0$), and friction constantly drains that energy away ($\dot{V} < 0$), the system must eventually stop moving and settle down at rest. The beauty of this method is that you prove stability without ever solving the differential equations!

💡 Understand It Simply (Manglish) — Local Campus Analogy
🌴 Local Tone ▼

Oru dynamic system-te total stored energy aanu $V(x)$. System stable aavan: Energy eppozhum positive aayirikkannam ($P > 0$), pinne samayam pokumthorum energy kuranju kuranju varanam ($\dot{V} = -Q < 0$, dissipation). Friction pole energy consume cheythu system rest-ilekk ethum!
